The study carried out by Brand Finance Football in May 2018 ranked Olympique Lyonnais in 32° place among European
brands. Amid rankings dominated by numerous English clubs, the Olympique Lyonnais brand came in with a value of € 145
million, making it the second most valuable French brand after Paris Saint-Germain (€748 million] and ahead of AS Monaco

(£107 million).

French market

In the French market, revenue excluding transfers for all
of Ligue 1 totalled €1.863 billion in 2016/17, up more than
10% from the previous year. This growth was naturally
driven by the increase in media rights, which stood at
€935 million, vs €761 million in 2015/16. Besides the
25% contractual increase in national rights, media rights
related to European play increased 19%, mainly due to

the participation of AS Monaco in the Champions League
and Olympique Lyonnais in the Europa League, for a total
of €195 million in 2016/17 (vs €151 million in 2015/16).

The growth in revenue was also the result of a 5% increase
to €390 million in sponsorship receipts. Attracting an
increasingly broad range of sponsorships, and driven by
foreign investments, many clubs have continued their
efforts to diversify their revenue.

Transfers to and from abroad

Ligue 1 sales to foreign clubs totalled €260.4 million
(51 transfers), while Ligue 1 acquisitions from abroad
totalled €260 million (58 transfers), generating a positive
balance of €0.4 million.

Transfers between French clubs

Ligue 1 sales to Ligue 2 totalled €0.2 million (3 transfers),
while Ligue 1 acquisitions from Ligue 2 totalled €12.7
million (15 transfers), generating a negative balance of
€12.5 million. Intra-Ligue 1 transfers (21) totalled €49.0
million.

The general transfer balance was €-12.1 million this
season (vs €147.2 million in 2015/16).

Winter 2018 transfer window (source: LFP)

In total, the volume of transactions was relatively stable
compared to the trends seen in previous seasons.

Of the 160 transfers, vs 165 the previous year, this winter
transfer season included 38 transfers of free agent players
{vs 31 in 2017), 56 permanent transfers (61 in 2017) and
66 loans (73 in 2017).

As in the two preceding seasons, transactions between
French clubs constituted the majority of transfers
completed in January. However, the number of players
going abroad increased: 36 additions from abroad (51 in
2017), 60 departures abroad (51 in 2017] and 64 transfers
between French clubs (63 in 2017).

Expenditure on definitive transfers between French clubs
increased once again (€24 million vs € 21 million in 2017).
Additions to French clubs from abroad represented €32
million {vs €156 million in 2017, which was an exceptional
year) and departures abroad were €58 million [vs €19
million in 2017). Four transactions (departures/additions
to the squad) exceeded €10 million, including two for
Monaco and one for Lyon. The French clubs had a transfer
surplus this season of €26.6 million [vs €-137 million for
the 2017 winter transfer window and €+10.1 million for the
2016 winter transfer window.

6. BUSINESS OVERVIEW

Summer 2018 transfer window (source: LFP)

During the 2018 summer transfer window, the number
of transfers remained stable (579 vs 572 in 2017), but the
amounts of transfers increased by 10% compared to 2017
(£1,265 million vs €1,153 million in 2017). This change was
primarily related to an increase in the amounts of trans-
fers related to the departure of players to foreign leagues
{amount up 40% compared to 2017, while the number
of transfers increased only 6%, confirming the growing
attractiveness of players in France). Overall, the French
clubs had a substantial transfer surplus of €347 million,
significantly higher than in other leagues.

Arrivals from abroad represented €287 million vs €534
million in 2017. Monaco and Lyon still have substan-
tial transfer surpluses of €210 million and €57 million,
respectively. PSG and Marseille are the two clubs that
spent the most during the 2018 summer transfer window.
Their transfer balances were €-84 million and €-23
million, respectively.

6. BUSINESS OVERVIEW

6.6 REPORT ON CORPORATE SOCIAL
RESPONSIBILITY

Olympique Lyonnais is a responsible and
committed corporate citizen

Olympique Lyonnais is pursuing its CSR objectives by
integrating responsibility, solidarity and engagement
issues into the Group's overall strategy. This is a long-term
initiative which fosters a productive dialogue and collab-
oration with all stakeholders on economic, social and
environmental aspects of Olympique Lyonnais’ business.

Statement from Jean-Michel Aulas, Chairman

of Olympique Lyonnais

“Social and environmental responsibility is a concern
shared by all of the Club's constituents. The policy for
sport, educational and citizen-ship training implemented
by our Academy, our commitment to promoting and devel-
oping women's football and the social initiatives under-
taken by OL Fondation illustrate our desire to innovate in
these areas.

Groupama Stadium and the development of OL City
are clearly part of this ambition, which required taking
the economic, environmental and social aspects into
consideration in developing these projects. In April 2018,
Olympique Lyonnais signed the “Charter of environmen-
tally responsible commitments” supported by the Ministry
of Sports and the WWF France. Through this commitment,
as operator of Groupama Stadium and organiser of major
events, we want to take a progressive approach to environ-
mental protection, accessibility and solidarity for all the
events organised at Groupama Stadium.”

Statement from Sidonie Mérieux, Chairwoman

of the CSR Committee of Olympique Lyonnais’ Board

of Directors

"This season marks the 10‘ anniversary of the OL Fonda-
tion, 10 years of engagement supporting nonprofit leaders
in the region. OL Fondation is very much a part of the
Club's CSR strategy, with the desire to innovate, mobilise
all of our stakeholders and make our initiatives last for
the long-term.

Our ambition is to position Groupama Stadium as a centre
of social innovation and to develop social and environ-
mental partnerships to create something that does not
exist in any other stadium in France or Europe.

The Corporate and Employment Centre and the develop-
ment of ODAS (Open Data Application for Skills) testing
perfectly embody this ambition through a new recruiting
approach that focuses on candidates’ skills and potential
rather than their CV.

Following this initial success, we will create new projects
to make OL City a place for everyday life, driven by a social
dimension, that will help develop eastern Lyon.”

1/ Olympique Lyonnais is a responsible club

the staff to develop and by attracting additional talent to
respond to new needs.

Remuneration policy

The Group's remuneration policy is characterised by the
following distinction:

e The remuneration of administrative employees is split
into fixed and variable portions. The variable portion is
dependent on whether certain qualitative and quantita-
tive objectives specific to each type of work are achieved.
Variable pay, particularly as it relates to employees in
sales positions, is a mechanism that fosters the Group's
business development.

+ For players and coaches, remuneration is negotiated
between the Club and the player or coach. Most player
salaries are divided into a fixed portion and a variable
portion based on the player's individual performance and/
or the team's collective performance.

The remuneration policy is complemented by collective
measures intended to motivate employees, based in part
on the performance of the Group.

Gross payroll over the last three financial years (in € 000):

in € 000! | 2017/18) 2016/17)  2015/16
Consolidated gross payroll 85,158 81,664 73,585

The €3.5 million increase in the payroll is due principally
to (i] an increase in the men's team's payroll (bonuses
on sporting results as a result of the 3" place ranking in
Ligue 1 this season, vs 4‘ place in the 2016/17 season,
qualifying the Club for the Champions League vs Europa
League in the 2016/17 season), and {ii] an increase in the
fixed component of remuneration in the women's team's
payroll.

The increase in the administrative payroll is the result of
new hires and raises related to the performance of the
administrative teams. There was no profit-sharing reserve
for this season; a collective bonus was paid, in accordance
with the new Group agreement implemented as of the
2017/18 season.

Working hours and absenteeism
In general, working time is organised as follows:

+ Administrative personnel have standard office working
hours,

e Certain departments, whose activities depend on match
schedules or more generally are event-based, have
individualised schedules [ticketing, security, shops, TV
channel, etc.).

Players and coaches also have individualised schedules
to take into account the timing of competitions and the
players’ training and physical preparation that must be
adapted toit.

1.1.2 Labour-management dialogue

During the 2017/18 season, OL Groupe and OL Organisa-
tion held employee elections 50 as to seat a single delega-
tion of personnel representatives.

The Group now has 18 principal representatives and eight
alternates. They represent all personnel in the Group and
its subsidiaries and ensure ongoing labour-management
dialogue. There is dialogue in all areas in the Group, asthe
sporting segment also has elected player representatives.

promoting employee well-being (sport classes, informal
evening events, company concierge).

Social and cultural activities are managed collectively
by the inter-company works Council, to which the Group
contributed €459 thousand for the 2017/18 season.

1.1.3 Health and safety policy

In accordance with its obligations, the Group introduced
a combined risk evaluation document, so as to better
evaluate risks by business activity. This document is
regularly updated.

The Group has also appointed a safety manager in charge
of monitoring and updating this document, in consultation
with personnel representatives. Health and safety issues
are discussed regularly with the relevant personnel repre-
sentatives.

No collective agreement has been signed with regard to
health and safety in the workplace.

Contrary to the data for the men's and women's profes-

there was an increase in the number of lost days for

In addition, developments in medical care initiated since
the 2014/15 season continue to benefit all of the teams:

- The special food-service system implemented for the
members of the Olympique Lyonnais professional men's
team is dedicated to OL's women's team as well:

- The GPS player monitoring for individualising each
player's workload based on his or her physical condition
is now used for academy players as well;

- The implementation of prevention protocols for muscle
damage (hamstrings and adductors) and individualisation
of these protocols has reduced muscle damage in all
teams;

- Management has advanced: daily on-site ultrasound
diagnosis and monitoring, infiltration, early muscle
recovery.

This work, led by OL's medical team, has resulted in a clear
improvement in player availability.

1.1.4 Training programmes

With France moving to deregulate professional training,
the Club has instituted a plan to optimise its training
budgets to address the need for developing employee
skills.

The 2017/18 training programme helped to achieve these
objectives by expanding the notion of what constitutes
training, simplifying the process of identifying all training
activities and encouraging co-investment on the part of
the employee and the Company in implementing training
programmes.

METTENT ME TU

Total number of training hours 10,190 8,351 7,032
Percentage of payroll devoted to 032% 031% 037%
training

Average number of training days per 7 7 8

employee

The efforts undertaken by the Club to deploy this skills
development programme are focused on two objectives:
to develop talent and renew the culture of service.

Many initiatives are implemented to attract and retain
employees who are motivated and involved in a team
culture of mutual respect and trust. As an example, a
broad-scale training programme is being rolled out for
managers, with the major goal of improving all levels of
internal communication.

Positions combining study and apprenticeship are playing
an increasingly important role at Olympique Lyonnais,
because this method of training contributes to the quality
of future hires. Furthermore, OLSs training body received
the approval of the Auvergne-Rhône-Alpes region and
the sports apprenticeship training centre to open an
apprenticeship section for eight young players from the
OL Academy beginning in August 2018. This made-to-
measure curriculum will allow them to obtain a football
instruction certificate {Brevet de Moniteur de Football)
and an equivalency with regard to the football coaching
diploma known as the "BP JEPS”. The total budget
allocated via the apprenticeship tax is around €82,000.

The training team is getting more and more involved with
supporting employee training initiatives: resuming studies,
conversions and the desire for certified training are always
viewed positively, to the extent possible considering
staffing constraints. In particular, the support offered to
the sports professionals has been strengthened: € 73,000
has been invested in training women players this season.
Seven women players completed training that allowed
them to acquire the equivalent of a “Bac +2" diploma {high
school plus two years’ post-secondary]. Integrating foreign
players has been accelerated by systematically providing
courses in French as a foreign language. Training to obtain
an educator diploma issued by the French football federa-
tion, media training using the OL TV premises, internships
to learn about different professions and the opportunity to
complete a diploma online were all implemented during
the season. More than 3,600 hours of training have been
provided for sports players and staff, with an overall
budget allocation of more than €127,000.

Lastly, the training activities and other measures put in
place internally enable Olympique Lyonnais personnel
to exchange ideas, share best practices and help them

6. BUSINESS OVERVIEW

to standardise the quality of service provided by all
employees, in line with the Club's fundamental values.

For services offered externally, OLSs training body worked
in detail on the content of its partnerships with local and
Paris-based clubs: 17 training programmes were held
during the season over 39 days for 165 licenced players
from these clubs. This experience also allowed the
training organisation to be retained for areas in which it
has positioned itself, as part of the national call for tenders

actions helped to promote the OL brand, especially in the
OL internships with the inclusion of an offer of OL training
for the internship supervisors offered by Cap Junior. They
also helped to bring in new customers (FOCAL, CFA, etc.).

These efforts will continue as an ongoing improvement
initiative for administrative processes, in particular to
harmonise and streamline the departures for training on
the entire OL Groupe scale.

1.1.5 Equal treatment

Equal status for men and women

Professional equality between men and women is
maintained in terms of recruitment, employee status and
internal promotion, while taking into account the specific
nature of the Group's business.

The Club has both men's and women's teams, with male
and female players both in training and under profes-
sional contracts. The men's and women's professional
teams train at the Groupama OL Training Center, which
opened in July 2016 in the Groupama Stadium complex.
The Groupama OL Academy, in operation since August
2016, is the first mixed-sex training academy in France.

Employment and integration of people with disabilities
into the workforce

The Group has seven employees with disabilities, including
three under permanent employment contracts. The
Company's total contribution to AGEFIPH, the organisa-
tion that manages funds devoted to integrating people with
disabilities, was €49,700 for the 2017/18 season.

More generally, all forms of discrimination are strictly
prohibited by the Group's internal regulations.

1.2 Integrity

1.2.1 Promoting fundamental rights and principles
in the workplace

The Group is committed to promoting and adhering to
the terms of the International Labour Organisation's
fundamental agreements on respecting the freedom to
associate and the right to collective negotiation, elimi-
nating job discrimination and forced labour, and abolishing
child labour.

The Group also adheres to the principles of the French
Labour Code and includes the main principles of this
Code in the internal regulations of the Group and of its
various subsidiaries. These principles reaffirm the Group's
commitments related to working conditions, health and
safety standards, and measures to combat all forms of
harassment and discrimination.

The Group commits to promoting all of the above princi-
ples vis-à-vis all of its stakeholders: employees, suppliers,
partners and customers.

1.2.2 Combating corruption, betting
and unfair practices

Football is not exempt from corruption, and various
highly-publicised scandals have cast a spotlight on the
problem. Conscious of the existence of corruption and
other illicit practices undermining sports and society at
large, Olympique Lyonnais has developed a number of
tools to understand them and bring them under control.
The Group's internal regulations defend certain funda-
mental principles and warn all employees against unscru-
pulous practices that could harm the Company:

- The internal regulations warn all employees against
accepting gifts or tips from customers or suppliers; a
paragraph included therein reminds them of the regula-
tory and legal framework.

- Players and employees are especially made aware of
the prohibition on sport betting. These principles are
repeated orally at the player's pre-recruitment inter-
view and at contract signing. Checks are carried out on
Olympique Lyonnais players, as they are on all other
players, in cooperation with ARJEL and the LFP in their
efforts to identify players who have placed bets. AL OL
professional players under contract have been found to
be in compliance with regulations. As part of the sponsor-
ship agreement between Olympique Lyonnais and the
FDJ, a programme to increase awareness of and prevent
risks related to gambling in general, and sports bettingin
particular, will be organised for administrative employees,
players and sport staff during the 2018/19 financial year: a
similar initial initiative was conducted several years ago.

- Players and employees are also warned against doping
and/or the use of harmful substances. Players agree to
submit to anti-doping tests when they are requested to
do so. The Group tolerates no exception to this rule and
tells players they must submit to these tests willingly and
immediately. In this regard, OL players were tested for
doping during the 2017/18 season. Alltests were negative.

- The Group also monitors payments to the various stake-
holders at the time of player transfers 50 as to avoid any
money laundering or other irregularities in the movement
of funds.

- Lastly, in compliance with law no. 2016-1691 of 9
December 2016 [the Sapin 2 law), OL Groupe has imple-
mented a whistleblower system that allows personnel
to report suspicious activity and guarantees that their
reports are handled confidentially.

6. BUSINESS OVERVIEW

1.2.3 Data protection

The Olympique Lyonnais Groupe has always sought to
ensure the protection, confidentiality and security of
personal data which may be entrusted toit, and especially
its customer/supporter, player, sport staff and employee
data. Protecting private lives is an essential ethical and
commercial pre-requisite for establishing and maintaining
a relationship of trust with stakeholders, and for offering
new opportunities to bring additional value to the
Company.

In compliance with the General Data Protection Regula-
tion, which became effective on 25 May 2018, Olympique
Lyonnais Groupe has strengthened its policy. It has desig-
nated a data protection representative and a dedicated
unit in which the IT systems department participates.
Various compliance projects have been identified, prior-
itised and are being carried out (technical and/or legal
analysis, document drafting, implementation). Everyone
required to handle personal data is made aware of his or
her responsibilities, through internal procedures, informa-
tion meetings for employees, and a code of good conduct
for company subcontractors. À data protection charter,
distributed to all customers and published on OL's website,
describes in more detail Olympique Lyonnais’ commit-
ments with regard to personal data protection and how to
exercise personal rights.

1.2.4 Ensuring spectator safety

The LFP's football match organisation charter stipulates
that it is incumbent upon the club that hosts the match to
implement all organisational and safety measures neces-
sary for the event. This rule is based in part on the French
Sports Code and calls for various safety and security
measures to be adopted.

In response, the Group's internal stadium security plan
specifies the staff who must be present for each match
(welcome, security, etc.], depending on the level of risk
attributed to the match.

During the 2017/18 season, Groupama Stadium hosted 28
matches organized directly by Olympique Lyonnais:

- 19 Ligue 1 matches, including 2 ranked level 5 (Paris
and Nice) and 1 ranked level 3 on the stadium's Internal
Security Plan;

- 5 European cup matches for the men's team, including 1
ranked level 5 (Everton) and 3 ranked level 3:

- 1 women's Division 1 match:

- 3women's Champions League matches.

À total budget of €5,913 thousand was devoted to the
security and safety measures during these 28 matches.

The significant decline in costs compared to the 2016/17
season was due to:

- four fewer men's team matches:

- the dropin the risk level for certain matches (Olympique
de Marseille and Saint-Étienne]. The prefectural order
prohibiting the transport of visiting spectators caused
these two matches to be classified as level 4, which
resulted in the immediate reduction of both Club and
national police security forces;

- close dialogue and collaboration with governmental
departments (prefecture and Minister of the Interior),
making it possible to better adapt the security services
employed for each match:

- optimised security and welcome personnel based on
experience from the 2016/17 season.

At the same time, the experience from last season led the
Group to make adjustments and acquire new resources
for the purpose of increasing security for matches. These
provisions limited the number of major incidents within
the stadium's perimeter and the stadium itself.

There were nevertheless two significant events during the
2017/18 season:

- the OL-0M match with a large number of fireworks set
off at the north end of the stadium to mark the anniversary
of a supporter group,

- the OL-Moscow Europa League match with clashes
between fans and security forces outside the stadium's
perimeter. Video surveillance images of the stadium were
provided to the security forces and helped to identify some
of the offenders.

Although neither the LFP nor UEFA still requires clubs to
send representatives to control fans during away matches,
Olympique Lyonnais continues to manage them. Overall
security costs for away matches during the 2017/18
season totalled €125 thousand (€135 thousand for the
2016/17 season).

OL GROUPE Financial Year 17/18

--- pág. 77 ---

6. BUSINESS OVERVIEW

This decline in costs was mainly the result of:

- for Championship matches, several prefectural orders
for controlling supporters at away matches, or totally
prohibiting their attendance;

- for European Cup play, fewer supporters in attendance
because of the clubs visited and the modes of transport
offered.

Lastly, the responsibility of the Club during major events
held at Groupama Stadium depends on how they are
managed:

- if Olympique Lyonnais is the organiser or co-organiser,
security arrangements are managed by the Club;

- for other major events, Olympique Lyonnais provides
consulting services and communicates information about
the venue. The Club's responsibility is therefore limited
to making the stadium available and ensuring that it
complies 24/7 with building fire and safety codes.

1.2.5 Responsible purchasing policy

CSR is one of the mainstays of Olympique Lyonnais”
purchasing policy, which is carried out with all of OL
Groupe's support functions. For occasional purchases or
more complex purchases governed by calls for tenders,
service providers are selected in part on CSR criteria,
such as whether they carry out initiatives aimed at helping
people enter or re-enter the workforce, train people with
specific personal or professional problems, promote local
employment, work to better manage waste, or undertake
measures with our food-service partners to limit waste.

À year ago, the purchasing department implemented a
service provider “scorecard”, i.e. a table of indicators to
measure, objectively and factually, action taken to improve
the quality of the service or product, the quality of informa-
tion and assistance, and plans for ongoing improvement
toward excellence. The goal is to expand this measure-
ment of supplier performance to all major suppliers.

1.3 The OL Academy is forward-looking and

conscious of its responsibility
The regular integration of young Academy players into the
professional team and the outside interest they attract
indicate the dynamic, good quality of the training.

Twelve of the 26 players on the professional squad during
the 2017/18 season had been trained at the OL Academy.
Among them, Nabil Fékir, Anthony Lopes and Houssem
Aouar were instrumental in producing the results obtained
throughout the season, which enabled the club to reach
the Europa League round of 16 and obtain a 22° consecu-
tive qualification for European cup play and, in particular,
a 16* qualification for the Champions League. At the same
time, Academy teams distinguished themselves by their
competitiveness in their own leagues, by the young players
represented in the national team and by the quality of
their play.

For the sixth consecutive year, Olympique Lyonnais ranked
first among training academies in France, and it ranked
third in Europe. These rankings are based on the number
of young players trained at the Club [from Category U8]
and who sign a professional contract [one in six youths at
OL vs an average of one in 10 at nationwide); those who
play on a first team or on a national team; the number of
youths who earn diplomas or gain experience during their
training; and the number of coaches.

The Olympique Lyonnais Academy continues to grow to
achieve its high expectations in terms of sport training and
education, thanks to the allocated resources. To achieve
the desired results, the Academy maintains a five-level
training policy:

- constant investment in all the Academy's activities,
aimed at success in sports and education;

- ongoing analysis to capitalise on the Academy's
strengths and add value to the sport training and educa-
tion it provides;

- human resource development to raise the level of skills
while preserving morale and values, the cornerstones of
success and performance;

- diligent monitoring so as to be up to date on the devel-
opment of new methods for learning, on news in the area
of training and on the plans of other training academies
in France, other European countries and the rest of the
world;

- vision to anticipate changes in the game, in society and
in training.

Although the training academy's major objectives are
aimed at offering the professional team young players
who are able to compete at the European and international
levels, some youths will not achieve this athletic Level.
Thanks to the training programmes three commitments
to the success of young players athletically, scholastically
and socially, the majority of them will succeed at interme-
diate levels, or in other professions, and they will all have
fond memories of OL's identity and philosophy.

6. BUSINESS OVERVIEW

2] Olympique Lyonnais demonstrates solidarity

Olympique Lyonnais has a corporate foundation, OL
Fondation, and an endowment fund, sOLidarity ["sOLid-
aire"). The objective of these two funds is to embody the
Club's values of solidarity and commitment and to play an
active role in regional development.

2.1 OL Fondation levels all playing fields

through solidarity
Created in 2007, OL Fondation has been supporting the
development of public interest projects organised by
the social and solidarity-based economy for more than
10 years. The foundation's initiatives are based on three
commitments:

- Draw on the skills and legitimacy of the nonprofit milieu;
- Forge long-term partnerships to optimise social impact;

- Mobilise Olympique Lyonnais players 50 as to throw a
spotlight on the projects and integrate their sponsors into
a network.

To gain efficiency and coherency, the foundation's work for
2017/18 was refocused around two major partnerships:

- The Léon Bérard centre for healthcare;

- Sport dans la Ville and social integration through sports.

The 2017/18 year was also marked by various events
organised to recognise the foundation's 10-year anniver-
sary:

- a Ligue 1 match dedicated to this anniversary, with an
advertising campaign;

- a book entitled “70 ans OL Fondation” (OL Fondation
10 years) and a 52-minute documentary;

- a gala dinner for partner companies;

- a call for projects from Club supporters and which led
to the outlay of € 200,000 for 40 nonprofit projects in the
Auvergne-Rhône-Alpes region, in areas such as education,
health, culture, sport and social assistance. Promoting
social engagement by its supporters while relying on the
collective donations of OL's two professional teams made
this call for projects a totally innovative programme for
both French and European football.

Following an initial cycle of five years, OL Fondation was
extended three times, in 2012, 2015 and again in 2018,
each time for a three-year period.

For the 2017/18 season, OL Fondation's budget, including
all types of donations, totalled €281.9 thousand.

Created in 2009, the sOLidarity fund complements OL
Fondation's initiatives by contributing occasional or
regular support to associations and initiatives. One of
the driving forces behind the creation of the sOLidarity
fund was to make it easier for the partners and sponsors

of Olympique Lyonnais to make a commitment. For the
2017/18 season, sOLidarity's budget, including all types
of donations, totalled €1,567 thousand. This budget was
increased considerably in 2017/18 (£536.5 thousand) to
finance the development of new projects related to the
community innovation centre and events organised as part
of OL Fondation's 10-year anniversary.

2.2 Building a community innovation centre

2.2.1 Making an impact on the ground and creating ties
with stakeholders

With the completion of Groupama Stadium, the Club's
social policy became oriented around developments in
the business world to position the stadium as a commu-
nity innovation centre, a place for everyday life, enlivened
by a social dimension. Olympique Lyonnais has made the
stadium into a resource centre open to everyone, helping
to promote the sense that Groupama Stadium belongs to
the local community. It also constitutes an opportunity
to establish connections with the various stakeholders:
institutions, OL Business Team partners, supporters, local
residents and associations.

Employment was the first topic to be discussed as part of
the community innovation centre. The aim is to stimulate
Local employment with the help of Olympique Lyonnais’
corporate network. Partnerships have also been devel-
oped with local communities to encourage entrepre-
neur-ship and support the nonprofit ecosystem. WAOUP,
Ronalpia and the Lyon branch of “Cravate Solidaire”
stand out as examples. Groupama Stadium's facilities for
accommodating people with disabilities are exemplary.
They have been specially designed and implemented to
facilitate access for spectators: seat reservation through a
dedicated box office, easy access to the stadium, dedicated
seating areas, as well as restaurant services, access to
VIP areas, restrooms and shops that accommodate people
with disabilities. The stadium won the 2016 “Disabled
Spectator Prize” for accessibility, granted by the French
Professional Football League and Française des Jeux.

In future, the Club aims to develop other innovative
projects around Groupama Stadium, in particular in the
areas of disability, culture and sport/health/well-being.

2.2.2 The “Corporate and Employment Centre”

The Club is determined to extend the employment
momentum created by the construction and delivery of
Groupama Stadium. To this end, it has created a “Corpo-
rate and Employment Centre” in partnership with the
national employment office. The goal of this initiative is
to identify employers’ needs, and in particular the needs
of the companies that are OL partners, so as to have a
tangible impact on professional integration and employ-
ment. This project, rolled out on 6 October 2016, continued
throughout the 2017/18 season. It is a platform dedicated

OL GROUPE Financial Year 17/18

--- pág. 79 ---

6. BUSINESS OVERVIEW

to employment in the heart of the stadium, the first of its
kind in Europe. It takes an innovative approach, relying on
companies to recruit locally and on a network of social and
solidarity economy participants.

Since its inauguration in October 2016, the Corporate and
Employment Centre has:

- organised 33 recruiting events (job-dating or job forums],

- hosted 136 companies that posted more than 1,200 job
offers,

- helped bring about more than 1,000 new recruitments.

The Corporate and Employment Centre is also being used
as a testing ground for ODAS, an experiment organised
by the sOLidarity endowment fund, Campus Veolia and
the French national employment office as part of the
French government's Future Investment Programme.
The purpose of this experiment is to foster professional
mobility and boost employment in a region by creating
synergies between the various participants by establishing
a common language with regard to skills.

The members of the consortium are committed to three
deliverables:

- Developing a skills language applicable to all business
sectors by observing actual work;

- Installing software that can determine the needs of
companies and the profiles of candidates interested in
mobility;

- Implementation of a planner, a 3D visualisation tool that
locates the skill requirements and resources in a region.

The construction of Groupama Stadium was an opportu-
nity to create jobs for people having difficulty integrating
the workforce: 17% of the hours worked on the new
stadium site involved people who qualified for assistance
(< 26 years old, > 50 years old, social welfare recipients,
long-term unemployed, etc.).

Job creation continued when Groupama Stadium entered
service. With more than 80 service providers and 1,800 to
2,200 people working on event days, Olympique Lyonnais
indirectly helped create more than 1,000 other jobs that
were filled between June 2015 and June 2016. Ultimately,
with the construction of related facilities (hotel, office
buildings, Leisure & entertainment centre, medical centre)
Groupama Stadium will create numerous additional
indirect jobs.

3/ Olympique Lyonnais is a committed corporate citizen

3.1 Committed to developing women's football

The Club was a precursor in women's football, having
created a female section in 2004. It gradually built an
extremely competitive team on the national and then inter-
national level. This season, the OL women's team made

more Champions League and European football history
when they won their fifth UEFA Women's Champions
League title, and their third consecutive one [after 2011,
2012, 2016 and 2017). Domestically, the OL women won
their 12 straight women's Division 1 title.

Owing to their high-quality play and the high-quality facil-
ities that now host their matches, the OL women's team
are attracting ever more spectators, in particular for
major Division 1 matches and for the Champions League
matches played at Groupama Stadium.

The women's team is also well-represented in interna-
tional squads, notably in the French national side, and
confirms its dominant position in French and European
women's football. OL Women's team members have
been selected to play on the German, Japanese, English,
Canadian and Dutch national teams, making OL women's
football a key benchmark. Some of its players could very
well be back on the Groupama Stadium pitch for the
semi-finals and final of the 2019 FIFA Women's World
Cup, to be held in France.

With its mixed-sex training academy, inaugurated in
August 2016, OL Academy confirmed its status as the
leading training academy in France for the sixth consec-
utive year and demonstrated once more its willingness to
commit to the long-term promotion and development of
women's sport.

The female section already has a significant place in
the Academy, as girls account for more than 30% of all
OL-licensed young players. This is well ahead of the
national average and reflects the Club's determination
to develop football for both sexes by throwing a spotlight
on women's football and enabling girls to join France's
amateur clubs.

3.2 Groupama Stadium is committed to protecting
the environment

3.2.1 The Group's general environmental policy

Although its activities pollute very Little by nature (enter-
tainment and media sector), Olympique Lyonnais does its
utmost to prevent unwanted consequences that its activ-
ities could have on the environment. These efforts have
been strengthened since Groupama Stadium operation
began.

Groupama Stadium, inaugurated on 9 January 2016, is part
of an ambitious eco-responsible plan, strengthened during
2017/18 when OL signed the charter of 15 environmentally
responsible commitments established by the Ministry of
Sports and WWE France.

Sustainable development was taken into account right

6. BUSINESS OVERVIEW

from the design stage of the stadium, in particular via the
following imperatives:

-The need to ensure the environmental continuity of the
region in which it is located;

- The natural water cycles are maintained as best as
possible, by ensuring that the grounds are permeable so
that water can filter through and by storing rainwater for
reuse on site;

- Waste and energy are managed sustainably, through a
waste sorting system on the site and an energy strategy
that uses renewable sources, such as geothermal energy
and heat exchangers;

- Low-impact transport modes are promoted, by putting
priority on public transport and limiting car access, both
for security reasons and so as to reduce noise and conges-
tion;

- An anti-noise strategy has been implemented with the
help of ADEME for the purpose of measuring and limiting
noise pollution during events.

3.2.2 Groupama Stadium's environmental performance
in operation

Open every day for Group employees and for numerous
seminars, trade shows and corporate events, Groupama
Stadium accommodates up to 60,000 spectators and 2,200
workers during major events. The 45-hectare perimeter
includes the stadium as well as the training centre for the
professional teams. It has light treatment and heating
facilities for maintaining the pitch, a balneotherapy facility
for the players and numerous heating, ventilation, and air
conditioning systems covering the entire stadium. Besides
the Groupama Stadium pitch, the training centre includes
five natural grass pitches that are lighted and heated, and
a synthetic covered half-pitch.

Groupama Stadium's daily operation is subject to specific
monitoring by the Technical Department. Security of
individuals and the building, accessibility, comfort of users,
energy efficiency and operating costs are vital issues for
the Club.

Energy consumption

OL Groupe's energy consumption is considerable and
constitutes a strategic focus area within the Technical
Department. Since 2017, certain management tools have
been deployed to assess the major categories of water
and electricity consumption, with an eye to reducing them.
This management is becoming even more important since
consumption categories at Groupama Stadium are diversi-
fying: since the 2017/18 season, the training grounds have
also had a pitch heating system, for which the operating
time windows must be precisely configured.

Since Groupama Stadium entered service, 100% of the

construction of new renewable hydraulic, wind and solar
powered energy sources in France.

Over the medium term, Olympique Lyonnais is studying the

on the site, as well as another system of energy storage
so it can remove itself from the power grid during peak

Water consumption data showed a very sharp increase

Groupe's activities is very large and requires detailed
monitoring. To do this, the Technical Department will
deploy leak detection systems and valves to produce an
immediate reaction in case of a malfunction (water circuit-
breaker, pilot valves).

Waste management

On-going improvement in waste-management is one of
the stadium's operational objectives, given the volume of
waste generated, particularly on major event days. There
is a waste collection area in the stadium, with separate
skips for glass, bio-degradable items, ordinary industrial
waste, packaging and household waste. Additional means
of collection have been added since 2017, particularly
for paper, plastic and aluminium cans, and pallet wood.
The Club is working with the stadium's caterer and the
cleaning services provider to improve recycling of waste
collected from the stands after events and to continue
selective sorting in the hospitality areas.

Transport and accessibility

Groupama Stadium's system of spectator access favours
low-impact transport modes. There is a bicycle parking
area with 550 places, the T3 tram runs on an extension
right to the stadium entrance on event days, and free
shuttles are available from satellite car parks to reduce
traffic congestion and transport supporters in large
groups over the “last mile” to the stadium.

During the 2017/18 season, 43% of spectators came to
Groupama Stadium via public transport, the same propor-
tion as the previous season. Spectator modes of transport
are impacted by several factors: whether or not the tram
shuttles are running, the date and time of the event, the
stadium attendance rate, the type of audience [match or
concert, for example), etc. The percentage of spectators
travelling via public transport increases significantly when
the stadium is full. Conversely, for the women's team's
Champions League matches, when stadium parking is
free and the stadium is not as full, many spectators arrive
by car.

An audit of the Club's carbon footprint performed in 2010
showed that 95% of the greenhouse gas emissions related
to a football match at the Gerland stadium derived from
spectator transport. As there is no more recent carbon
footprint analysis, it is not possible to provide quantitative
information about greenhouse gas emissions produced
by Olympique Lyonnais. On scope 1 (direct emissions),
emissions are not material, given the Group's activi-
ties. Scope 2 emissions (indirect emissions associated
with the production of electricity] are also not material,
because 100% of the electricity consumed by Groupama
Stadium derives from a renewable source. Lastly, scope 3
emissions [indirect emissions related to the value chain)
are related principally to travel: spectator traffic on event
days, employee travel every workday and player travel
to away matches (for the players and staff of the men's
and women's professional teams and the OL Academy's
youth teams). In this regard, spectator travel has been
designed and organised to encourage public transport
and carpooling (2.8 persons per vehicle on average for
cars parked at the stadium). Employees and service
providers working daily on the site can also use public
transport. Olympique Lyonnais and some service providers
use electric vehicles for their movements within the site.
Within the next two years, when the hotel, office build-
ings, medical clinic and leisure & entertainment centre
adjacent to the stadium come into service, tram service
will be extended to the stadium every day, which should
increase the proportion of visitors and employees using
public transport. Lastly, the means of transport used by
players and staff - plane, train or bus - are determined by
the constraints of the match schedule and the distances
to be travelled.

Biodiversity

The measures to protect biodiversity that were initiated
in the construction phase with the Rhône département
section of the Rhône-Alpes Federation for the Protec-
tion of Nature (FRAPNA] have continued into the opera-
tional phase. An agreement provides a framework for
cooperation 50 as to minimise the impact on the environ-
ment, biodiversity, natural resources and the landscape
and provides for real compensation. In 2017/18, all the
tasks provided for under the agreement were carried
out [tracking of animal crossings, tracking of nocturnal
insects).

Lastly, six hives installed at Groupama Stadium are
furthering their goal of education and support for local
biodiversity. The hives are regularly maintained by a local
beekeeper, which allows children and other local visitors
to learn about bees through workshops.

4] Note on methodology and external auditors’ report

The companies affected by decree no. 2012-557 of 24 April
2012 must publish a report on their CSR activities.

At the end of the 2017/18 season, Olympique Lyonnais
Groupe satisfied this obligation once again by publishing
its non-financial, or CSR, report, in accordance with
the Grenelle Il environmental law. The Group provided
information on the report topics it deemed relevant. For
the other topics, the reasons they were not included are
indicated at the end of the chapter.

Organising data collection

Every year, reporting guidelines are used and updated so
as to reiterate reporting objectives, organise data collec-
tion and define the indicators. CSR correspondents are
identified in the relevant departments and a personis then
made responsible for consolidating the data and verifying
its consistency before publication. The CSR department
and the contributing departments meet to improve the
quality and relevance of the information communicated
every year.

The scope included all activities of Olympique Lyonnais
Groupe and its subsidiaries during the 2017/18 financial
year:

- The Human Resources department furnished a great
deal of the social indicators, which relate to all of the
Group's subsidiaries.

- Environmental indicators were handled by Administra-
tive Services and the Technical Department of Groupama
Stadium. The scope applicable to the use of resources
includes all sites held by the Group.

-Societal indicators were handled by a variety of partic-
ipants: Human Resources, OL Fondation, the sOLidarity
fund and Administrative Services.

Deloitte has verified all of the information contained in this
report, in accordance with the application decree of the
Grenelle 11 law. Deloitte's certification of the presence of
the CSR Information and its report on the fairness thereof
can be found at the end of this chapter.

Note on scope:

To distinguish between the various names used throughout
the report to indicate scope, the term ‘the Group” refers
to the company OL Groupe and all of its subsidiaries,
whereas “OL Groupe” refers specifically to the legal entity
bearing that name.

Indicators for which no information is provided

Environmental indicators:

+ resources devoted to preventing environmental risks
and pollution,

e measures to prevent, reduce or remediate pollutants
released into the air, water or soil that have a serious
impact on the environment,

e consumption of raw materials and measures in place to
use them more efficiently,

+ information and training for employees regarding
environmental protection,

e provisions and guarantees for environmental risks,
e adaptation to the consequences of climate change.

Olympique Lyonnais is essentially a service provider. As
such it is only marginally affected by the environmental
issues listed above.

Societal indicators:

e other initiatives undertaken, under 3] herein, in favour
of human rights.

At the national (French) level, no initiatives other than
those already described were deemed necessary.

INDEPENDENT VERIFIER’S REPORT ON THE SOCIAL,
ENVIRONMENTAL AND SOCIETAL INFORMATION
CONTAINED IN THE MANAGEMENT REPORT

Year ended 30 June 2018
To the shareholders,

As OL Groupe’s independent verifier, accredited by the
COFRAC [the French national accreditation body] under
number 3-104811), we hereby present to you our report
on the consolidated social, environmental and societal
information presented in the management report [herein-
after the “CSR Information”) for the financial year ended
30 June 2018, pursuant to Article L.225-102-1 of the
French Commercial Code.

Responsibility of the Company

It is the responsibility of the Board of Directors to prepare
a management report including the CSR Informa-
tion required under Article R.225-105-1 of the French
Commercial Code, in accordance with the guidelines used
{hereinafter the “Guidelines”] by the Company, which are
summarised in the management report and are available
upon request from the Company's headquarters.

Independence and quality control

Our independence is defined by regulations, the indus-
try's Code of Ethics, as well as the provisions of Article
L.822-11 of the French Commercial Code. In addition, we
have implemented a quality control system that includes
documented policies and procedures aimed at ensuring
adherence to ethics, professional standards and applicable
laws and regulations.

Responsibility of the independent verifier
It is our responsibility, based on our work, to:

- certify that the required CSR Information is presented
in the report or that the report includes an explanation of
their absence, pursuant to the third paragraph of Article
R.225-105 of the French Commercial Code (Certification
of the presence of CSR Information);

- express a conclusion of limited assurance that the CSR
Information, taken as a whole, is presented fairly, in all
significant respects, in accordance with the Guidelines
(Report on the fairness of the CSR Information).

It is not our responsibility to express our opinion on

[1] Scope available at www.cofrac.fr.

compliance with other legal provisions applicable to social,
environmental or anti-corruption matters.

We performed our review with a team of three people
over approximately one week in September 2018. To help
us carry out these tasks, we enlisted the support of our
experts in corporate social responsibility.

We conducted our engagement in accordance with profes-
sional standards applicable in France and with the decree
of 13 May 2013 stipulating how the independent verifier is
to carry out its remit, and concerning the fairness report,
in accordance with the ISAE 3000! international standard.

1. Certification of the presence of the CSR Information

Nature and scope of the work

We have examined, based on interviews with the managers
of the relevant departments, the Company's sustainable
development policies and the social and environmental
consequences of the Company's business activities, its
societal commitments and the programmes andinitiatives
resulting therefrom, if any.

We have compared the CSR Information presented in the
management report with the list provided in Article R.225-
105-1 of the French Commercial Code.

In the event certain consolidated information was omitted,
we verified that explanations were provided, in accordance
with the third paragraph of Article R.225-105 of the French
Commercial Code.

We have verified that the CSR Information covers the
consolidated scope, i.e. the Company and its subsidi-
aries, as defined in Article L.233-1, and the companies
it controls, as defined in Article L.233-3 of the French
Commercial Code, with the limitations specified in the
management report.

Conclusion

On the basis of our work, and taking into account the
limitations mentioned above, we certify that the required
CSR Information is presented in the management report.

2. Report on the fairness of the CSR Information

Nature and scope of the work

We conducted 10 interviews with the people in charge of
collecting the CSR Information from the departments and,
in certain cases, with those responsible for the internal
control and risk management procedures, so as to:

- assess the appropriateness of the Guidelines as regards
their relevance, completeness, reliability, neutrality and
clarity, taking into consideration, where applicable, the
sector's best practices;

- verify that the Company has implemented a procedure
for collecting, compiling, processing and verifying the

6. BUSINESS OVERVIEW

CSR Information so as to ensure its completeness and
consistency, and understand the internal control and risk
management procedures related to preparation of the
CSR Information.

We determined the nature and extent of our tests and
verifications based on the nature and importance of the
CSR Information, given the characteristics of the Company,
the environmental issues raised by the Company's
business activities, its sustainable development policies
and best practices in the industry.

For the CSR Information that we considered to be the most
important ll:

- at the level of the consolidating entity and the subsid-
iaries, we have examined documentation and held
interviews to corroborate the qualitative information
(organisation, policies, initiatives), applied analytical
procedures to quantitative information, verified, based on
sampling, the calculations and the consolidation of this
information, and verified its consistency with the other
information presented in the annual report;

- at the level of a sample of subsidiaries that we have
selected based on their business, their contribution to the
consolidated indicators, their location and a risk analysis,
we have conducted interviews to verify that procedures
were correctly applied, and we have performed detailed
tests based on sampling, consisting in verifying calcula-
tions and in reconciling data with supporting documents.
The sample covered all employees and all electricity
consumption indicated in this report 21.

Regarding the other consolidated CSR Information, we
assessed its consistency in relation to our knowledge of
the Company.

Lastly, we assessed the quality of the explanations
provided about any information that was partly or entirely
absent.

We believe that the sampling methods and the sample
sizes we used, while exercising our professional judge-
ment, enable us to formulate a conclusion of limited
assurance; a higher level of assurance would have
required more extensive verification work. Owing to the
use of sampling techniques and because of other limita-
tions inherent in any internal control and information
system, we cannot be entirely certain that no significant
anomaly in the CSR Information went undetected.

Conclusion

On the basis of our work, nothing has come to our atten-
tion to make use believe that the CSR Information is not
fairly presented, in all material respects, in accordance
with the Guidelines.

7.2 DESCRIPTION OF OL GROUPE'S PRINCIPAL
OPERATING SUBSIDIARIES

Olympique Lyonnais SASU

Olympique Lyonnais was incorporated in April 1992. Its
main purpose is to organise men's and women's profes-
sional team matches, and to manage the professional
teams by acquiring and selling players. Furthermore, it
promotes the Olympique Lyonnais brand through retailing,
marketing and distribution of derivative products relating
to the Club's business activity and produces television
programmes and corporate films, advertisements,
events-based programmes and documentaries. Foncière
du Montout was absorbed into Olympique Lyonnais as
of 30 June 2017, with retroactive effect to 1 July 2016.
Foncière du Montout's corporate purpose - to operate
the new stadium, and acquire, combine, develop, manage
and resell property units - was taken over by Olympique
Lyonnais. Groupama Stadium is now owned by Olympique
Lyonnais.

OL Organisation

Since it was created in June 2004, OL Organisation has
been providing, as its primary business, hospitality and
security services during various events and in particular
those related to the activities of Olympique Lyonnais.

Académie Médicale de Football

This company was formed on 15 October 2012 in the aim
of promoting Lyon's excellence in sports medicine. The
Group owns 51% of the share capital of Académie Médicale
de Football.

Beijing OL FC

This company was created on 9 March 2017. Its purpose is
to make Olympique Lyonnais more well-known, increase
its brand value and exploit its know-how (particularly in
player training] in the People's Republic of China, Hong
Kong, Macao and Taiwan.

The Group owns 45% of the share capital of Beijing OL
FC, with IDG European Sports Investment Ltd holding the
other 55%.

7. ORGANISATION CHART -— ISSUER'S POSITION IN THE GROUP AND LIST OF SIGNIFICANT SUBSIDIARIES

Other entities in the scope of consolidation

OL Association

OL Association includes the OL Academy, the women's first
team, as well as the male and female amateur sections.

Olympique Lyonnais SCI and Olympique Lyonnais
Mégastore SCI

OL Groupe also consolidated two property companies.
The assets of these two property companies were sold
during the 2016/17 financial year and the companies were
dissolved as of 30 November 2017.

OL Loisirs Développement/Too Fun Parc SCI

OL Loisirs Développement is a services and advisory
holding company created in July 2017. It holds a 5% stake
in Too Fun Parc SCI, which will manage the Leisure &
entertainment centre that will be part of OL City.

OL Partner

OL Partner is an insurance broker created in October 2017,
in partnership with Groupama Rhône-Alpes Auvergne.

Other entities related to the Group

Olympique Lyonnais Fondation

OL Fondation was created in 2007 for a five-year period
and extended a third time in 2018 for three years (2018/19
to 2020/21) by three of its founding members: OL Groupe,
OL SASU and Pathé Vaise. The foundation has a €405
thousand multi-year action programme to coordinate
social integration through sport, integration into the
workforce, education, assistance to sick and hospital-
ised people, and support for amateur sport. The founding
members can make additional in-kind contributions, such
as products or services to supplement the multi-year
action programme.

OL Fondation supports two major nonprofit organisations,
Sport dans la Ville and the Léon Bérard Center, and its
commitment to them is a long-term one. It also regularly
organises calls for projects to support other public interest
initiatives.

OL Fondation is not consolidated.

sOLidarity fund

On 17 November 2009, OL SASU and OL Fondation
created an endowment fund as provided for under the
“economic modernisation" Legislation (Act no. 2008-776 of
4 August 2008 and the application decree no. 2009-158 of
11 February 2009). Named “sOLidarity", the fund supple-
ments OL Fondation's initiatives by giving financial support
to various public interest projects through partnerships or
launching calls for projects.

The sOLidarity fund is not consolidated.

CENACLE

The sOLidarity fund and Association Olympique Lyonnais
are founding members of the Teaching and Assistance
Centre for Promoting Nonprofit Employment, known by
its French acronym as the "CENACLE" (Cité de l'Enseigne-
ment et de l'Accompagnement à la Création de L'Emploi
Associatif]. The CENACLE aims to develop training and
employability in nonprofit sports organisations and to train
their managers, employees and volunteers. Through its
contribution to the CENACLE, Olympique Lyonnais demon-
strates its determination to continue strengtheningits ties
with the nonprofit sector.

8. PROPERTY ASSETS

8.1 SIGNIFICANT PROPERTY, PLANT AND
EQUIPMENT, EITHER EXISTING OR PLANNED,
AND SIGNIFICANT EXPENSES RELATED TO THEM

8.1.1 Olympique Lyonnais sports complex

1/ Groupama Stadium

The new stadium entered service on 9 January 2016. Its
principal characteristics are as follows:

+ Capacity: ca. 59,000 people {including 6,000 VIP seats);
e Size: approx. 6 hectares (15 acres), housing:

- OL Groupe head office premises, located on
3,000 sq. m. of space within the stadium perimeter;

- The OL Store (approx. 830 sq. m.);
- A trophies room and a museum:

- À 51,486 sq. m. plaza that hosts various events and
constitutes a place for relaxation and enjoyment for all;

- 1,600 of the 6,700 parking spaces available on site,
underground.

The stadium represents €405.6 million in property, plant
& equipment on the balance sheet as of 30 June 2018.
This property, plant & equipment was initially recog-
nised at acquisition cost. The component approach was
then applied (construction, fittings, IT equipment, office
equipment, etc.) so as to depreciate the individual compo-
nents using the straightline method based on the Group's
estimation of the useful life of each component.

The principal operating costs of Groupama Stadium are
general maintenance expenses, upkeep of green spaces
and lawns, cleaning, IT maintenance, security and fluids
(electricity and water].

2/ Groupama OL Training Center

The construction of the new training centre for the profes-
sional team, with five pitches (one synthetic pitch and
a main pitch with 1,500 seats]) and an indoor, synthetic,
half-size pitch began in the 2014/15 financial year. The
training centre entered service in July 2016 and repre-
sented a total investment of €20.8 million.

3/ Groupama OL Academy

During the 2015/16 financial year, Association Olympique
Lyonnais launched the construction of the new
OL Academy building in Meyzieu, which was completed in
August 2016.

= $. PROPERTY ASSETS =

Construction of the new OL Academy resulted in a total
investment of €11.8 million.

4/ OL Museum

During the 2017/18 financial year, the Group inaugu-
rated an OL museum located in Groupama Stadium. The
museum represents an investment of €3.1 million.

9. FINANCIAL POSITION AND EARNINGS

AND EARNINGS

We invite you to read the following information relating to
the Group's financial position and earnings. The consol-
idated financial statements for the financial year ended
30 June 2018 have been prepared in accordance with IFRS
(standards, amendments and interpretations applicable in
the European Union as of 30 June 2018).

9.1 FINANCIAL POSITION AND CONSOLIDATED
OPERATING PROFIT, 2017/18 FINANCIAL YEAR

9.1.1 Comments on the consolidated income statement
for the year ended 30 June 2018

9.1.1.1 Total revenue

In 2017/18, revenue reached a record high of €289.5
million (up 16%), driven by player trading, which gener-
ated more revenue than ever before (€125.3 million] and
the Events business, which rose 70% to €15.6 million. The
year was also characterised by the Club's absence from
the Champions League, which reduced European ticketing
revenue and European media rights significantly.

Olympique Lyonnais’ men's team finished in third place
in the 2017/18 French Ligue 1 championship, thereby
qualifying directly for the group phase of the 2018/19
Champions League, and ranked for the 20* consecutive
time among the league's top five finishers. In European
Cup play, Olympique Lyonnais competed in the group stage
of the Europa League this season and reached the round of
16. The Club reached the quarter-final round of the Coupe
de France and the round of 16 in the Coupe de la Ligue.

The women's team won their 12 consecutive French

Division 1 championship title and their 5* UEFA Women's
Champions League title (3 consecutive).

Revenue reached a record high of €289.5 million in
2017/18, vs. €250.0 million in 2016/17, an increase of
€39.5 million, or 16%.

Revenue excluding player trading totalled € 164.2 million
in the 2017/18 financial year (€198.3 million in 2016/17).
As the Club did not participate in the Champions League
this season, ticketing and media rights revenue declined
by more than €40 million.

Revenue from the sale of player registrations was €125.3
million in 2017/18, the highest ever [vs €51.7 million in
2016/17)}, reflecting strong trading activity in a buoyant
European market. Revenue related to players trained
at the OL Academy totalled €90.1 million and included,
in particular, the transfers of Alexandre Lacazette and
Willem Geubbels. This confirmed once more the excel-
lent quality of the Academy, a source of value creation
and recurring business, as 72% of revenue from the sale
of player registrations related to players trained at the
Academy.

9. FINANCIAL POSITION AND EARNINGS

e Ticketing revenue

In 2017/18 ticketing revenue totalled €37.3 million, vs
€44.0 million in 2016/17, down €6.6 million or 15%,
reflecting the difference in the European tournaments in
which the men's professional team participated.

Ticketing revenue from French Ligue 1 matches increased
by €1.4 million to €30.9 million, vs €29.4 million in
2016/17. À new pricing strategy with a more versatile
range of ticket prices was implemented in October 2017,
boosting average attendance for Ligue 1 matchdays by
6,834 spectators to 46,005 in 2017/18, vs. 39,171 in 2016/17
and increasing related revenue by 5%.

Ticketing revenue from European matchdays totalled €6.3
million, vs €13.6 million in 2016/17 (i.e. a €7.3 million or
53% decrease). In 2017/18, it included ticketing revenue
from the Europa League group stage and round of 16. In
the previous season, the Club competed in the group stage
of the Champions League, then in the round of 32, round
of 16, quarter-final and semi-final of the Europa League.
In addition to the difference in competitions, European
ticketing revenue was also impacted by a lower number
of matches than in the previous season (4 vs 7).

Average revenue per matchday for all competitions
combined [men's professional team), including princi-
pally general public and VIP ticketing revenue and
merchandising revenue, totalled €1.8 million in 2017/18
(€£1.7 million in 2016/17), generating a gross margin once
again in excess of 50% (51.3% in 2017/18, vs 51.6% in
2016/17). Average matchday revenue per spectator, for all
competitions combined, was €40 (€44 in 2016/17).

e Sponsoring and advertising revenue

Sponsoring and advertising revenue totalled €30.1 million,
up €1.0 million or 4% from €29.1 million in 2016/17.
The three-year renewable Groupama Stadium naming
contract, which came into effect on 1 August 2017, offset
the decline or expiry of revenue from certain technology
and/or manufacturer contracts that had been imple-
mented during stadium construction.

+ Media and marketing rights

Media and marketing rights suffered significantly from the
Club's absence from the Champions League this season
and totalled €65.2 million in 2017/18, vs €98.9 million in
2016/17 (down €33.7 million or 34%).

Domestic media rights (LFP, FFF] totalled €51.0 million in
2017/18, vs €49.8 million in 2016/17 (up €1.6 million, or
3%), related in particular to the Club's 3rd-place finish in
the French Ligue 1 (4* in 2016/17).

International (UEFA) media rights totalled €14.2 million,
vs €49.6 million in 2016/17, i.e. down €35.4 million or 71%.
This came about because the Club did not participate in
the Champions League this season. They included receipts
related to the participation of the men's professional
team in the Europa League group stage and round of 16.

In the previous season, media rights related to the
Champions League group stage totalled €40.1 million,
and rights related to the Club's participation in the Europa
League up to the semi-final round totalled €9.5 million.

e Events

Created in January 2016 when the new stadium opened,
the new line item “Events” rose sharply in 2017/18, owing
in particular to a full schedule of prestigious events (excl.
OL matches).

During the course of the year, Groupama Stadium hosted
a Céline Dion concert (July 2017), a France-All Blacks
rugby friendly match (November 2017), the Europa League
final (May 2018), the two Top 14 semi-finals (May 2018), a
France-USA friendly match in the run-up to the World Cup
(June 2018] and a Monster Jam (June 2018).

The conventions, B2B seminars and corporate events
business continued to develop and represented €4.2
million, vs €4.1 million in 2016/17. Groupama Stadium
hosted 398 seminars (373 in 2016/17) and nearly 40,000
stadium visits.

e Brand-related revenue

At constant structure (excl. OL Voyages sold as of 30 June
2017), brand-related revenue continued to rise, reaching
€16.0 million, vs €14.5 million in 2016/17, an increase of
€1.5 million, or 10% (£17.1 million in 2016/17 including
OL Voyages).

Merchandising revenue totalled €10.6 million vs €9.6
million in 2016/17, up €1 million or 11% and up 56%
over three years. The internet became the leading sales
channel in 2017/18 with sales of €4.0 million [up 67% over
three years), pulling ahead of the stores, which posted
sales of €3.9 million in 2017/18 [up 35% over three years).
À new store was opened in Villefranche-sur-Saône in
November 2017.

° Revenue from sale of player registrations

Revenue from the sale of player registrations reached a
record high of €125.3 million in 2017/18 (£51.7 million in
2016/17).

It corresponded to the following transfers:

- Alexandre Lacazette to Arsenal (July 2017),

- Fahd Moufi to Tondela (July 2017),

- Maciej Rybus to Lokomotiv Moscow (July 2017),

- Emanuel Mammana to Zenit St. Petersburg
(July 2017),

- Gaëtan Perrin to Orléans (January 2018),

- Maxime d'Arpino to Orléans (January 2018),
- Alan Dzabana to Le Havre (January 2018),

- Willem Geubbels to AS Monaco (June 2018),
- Romain Del Castillo to Rennes (June 2018),

- Aldo Kalulu to FC Basel 1893 (June 2018),

- Jean-Philippe Mateta to Mainz (June 2018),

Total revenue from the sale of player registrations

Evidencing this expertise, Olympique Lyonnais received

The player trading business remains one of the Group's

EBITDA (2017/18: €73.9 million, 2016/17:€51.0 million)

EBITDA reached a record high of €73.9 million, up €22.8
million or 45% from 2016/17 and represented 26% of
total revenue. Since the new stadium opened during the
2015/16 season, EBITDA has averaged €59 million p.a.
over three years. It has been boosted by player trading,
which generated high capital gains on the sale of player
registrations (£110.6 million in 2017/18).

The payroll/revenue ratio, which stood at 40%, declined by
four points compared with the previous year, in line with
the Group's objective (<50%).

Personnel expenses totalled €115.0 million (£109.2

9. FINANCIAL POSITION AND EARNINGS

million in 2016/17]) and were inflated by bonuses tied to
sporting results, as the Club finished in 3" place in the
French Ligue 1 (4* in 2016/17] and qualified directly for
the 2018/19 Champions League season.

External purchases and expenses declined slightly (£0.7
million] and stood at €78.9 million vs €79.6 million in
2016/17. European match organisation costs declined,
because the tournament in which the Club competed was
different (Europa League vs Champions League), as was
the number of matches. Following the sale of OL Voyages
as of 30 June 2017, there were no purchases related to this
business during the 2017/18 financial year. Conversely, the
increase in Events and Merchandising revenue generated
a rise in the related expenses.

The market value of the men's professional team is still
very high, at €378.3 million as of 30 June 2018, vs €208.5
million as of 30 June 2017 (OL valuation based on Trans-
fermarkt and CIES). This valuation implies potential capital
gains of €297 million as of 30 June 2018 (£161.5 million as
of 30 June 2017), 49% of which related to players trained
at the OL Academy (67% as of 30 June 2017].

Profit from ordinary activities (2017/18: €25.2 million,
2016/17: €30.6 million)

Profit from ordinary activities exceeded €25 million in
2017/18 for the third consecutive year, at €25.2 million.

It was impacted by:

- An increase in net amortisation and provisions on player
registrations of €9.6 million (€23.3 million vs €13.7 million
in 2016/17), related to the trading that took place in the
summer of 2017.

- An unfavourable change in other ordinary income and
expenses. In 2016/17, other ordinary income and expenses
totalled €+10.7 million, reflecting the sale of property
assets in Gerland and a player insurance payment. In
2017/18, this line item totalled €-6.3 million and included
expenses related to player loans during the year.

Excluding the impact of other ordinary income and
expenses, profit from ordinary activities rose € 11.6 million
or 59% to €31.5 million in 2017/18, vs €19.9 million in
2016/17.

Net financial expense (2017/18: €13.3 million, 2016/17:
€23.2 million)

Net financial expense totalled €13.3 million, an improve-
ment of nearly €9.9 million compared with the previous
year (€23.2 million in 2016/17), and reflected in particular
the positive impact from refinancing nearly all of the
Group's debt. The refinancing was finalised in June 2017,
reducing the cost of debt by €6.4 million p.a. À one-time
expense of €2.7 million related to the refinancing also
impacted the previous year's financial expense.

10.3 BORROWING TERMS AND FINANCING
STRUCTURE

1/ Refinancing of virtually all of the bank and bond debt
as of 30 June 2017

On 30 June 2017, the Group finalised the refinancing of
virtually all of its bank and bond debt.

This refinancing was articulated around three debt instru-
ments granted to or issued by OL SASU:

1] a long-term bank credit agreement with an initial
amount of €136 million, divided into two tranches: (i] a
tranche À of €106 million, of which 50% amortises and
50% will be repaid at maturity in seven years; (ii] a tranche
B of €30 million to be repaid at maturity in seven years;

2) a €51 million bond issue, repayable at maturity in seven
years;

3] a five-year revolving credit facility (RCF] of €73 million,
available for short-term needs and renewable twice for
one year. The Group submitted its first one-year exten-
sion request as of 30 June 2018, which was unanimously
accepted by the bank lenders. The new expiry of the RCF
is thus 30 June 2023.

The three debt instruments granted to or issued by
OL SASU as of 30 June 2017 are governed by three ratios
applicable to the Group: {i] a gearing ratio {net debt to
equity] calculated every six months with a ceiling of 1.30,
declining to 1 starting on 31 December 2020, (ii) a Loan to
value ratio (net debt divided by the sum of the market value
of player registrations and the net book value of Groupama
Stadium, the training centre and the OL Academy) calcu-
lated every six months with a ceiling of 40%, declining to
35% starting on 31 December 2020, and iii) a debt service
coverage ratio calculated every six months on a rolling
12-month period, with a threshold of 1 (with the proviso
that if the ratio is Less than 1, it will be considered as met
if the cash on the Group's balance sheet, net of drawdowns
under the RCF and of any credit amount in the reserve
account, is greater than €20 million).

The lenders under these three debt instruments benefit
from a common set of security interests. Specifically, they
hold a first lien on the stadium, the land on which it was
built, the 1,600 underground parking spaces, the land
corresponding to the 3,500 outdoor parking spaces and
the areas leading to the stadium. In addition, the following
assets are pledged to the lenders: the shares OL Groupe
holds in OL SASU, certain bank accounts of OL SASU
and various receivables held by OL SASU on its debtors.
In addition, OL Groupe guarantees that its subsidiary
OL SASU will adhere to the obligations under its financing
arrangements.

The agreements related to these financing arrangements
include commitments on the part of OL SASU in the event
of accelerated maturity that are customary for this type of
financing. In particular, these include limits on the amount
of additional debt, cross default clauses and stability in the
shareholder structure of OL SASU and OL Groupe.

To reduce its exposure to interest rate risk under the €136
million long-term bank loan, OL SASU has maintained the
hedging programme it had implemented to cover the bank
loan that was refinanced on 30 June 2017. This hedging
programme had a notional amount averaging around
€97.4 million as of 30 June 2018.

Based on the €136 million long-term bank financing
and the €51 million bond issue, OL SASU should have
an average annual financing rate, from 30 June 2017, of
around 4.3%. This rate will depend on future changes in
benchmark rates.

2] BPI Loan

(See Note 9.7 to the consolidated financial statements)

As part of the financing of its businesses, OL Groupe took
out a Loan with BPI, a specialised financial institution,
during the 2013/14 financial year. The Loan has a face value
of €3 million and a seven-year maturity. The first repay-
ment was due on 30 June 2016. The loan has a retention
clause of €150 thousand.

3] Groupama Banque Loan

(See Note 12.4 to the consolidated financial statements]
The estimated total construction cost of the new training
centre and OL Academy is €30 million.

Financing for these investments was covered by:

+ a bank credit agreement signed by OL Groupe and OL
Association on 12 June 2015 in the amount of € 14 million
and with a 10-year maturity with Groupama Banque (now

Orange Bank). As of 30 June 2018, cumulative drawdowns
under these agreements totalled €8.9 million.

12. TRENDS

12.1 TRENDS SUBSEQUENT TO CLOSING

a/ Trends - events subsequent to closing

OL Groupe already has a very clear view of revenue growth
for the 2018/19 financial year, because: [i] it has qualified
for the Champions League group stage, which automati-
cally generates an increase in European ticketing revenue
and UEFA media rights; and (ii) total UEFA media rights
will increase 33% over the 2018-21 period.

Olympique Lyonnais was informed of the decision of UEFA
disciplinary commission to penalise the Club following
the OL-CSKA Moscow match last season. As a result, the
Champions League match against Shakhtar Donetsk on
2 October 2018 was played behind closed doors. A second
crowd ban has been suspended pending a two-year proba-
tionary period.
